,cr_item_qty/(sr_item_qty+cr_item_qty+wr_item_qty)/3.0 * 100 cr_dev        ,wr_item_qty        ,wr_item_qty/(sr_item_qty+cr_item_qty+wr_item_qty)/3.0 * 100 wr_dev Rewritten: with sr_items as (
    select i_item_id item_id,
           sum(sr_return_quantity) sr_item_qty
    from store_returns
    join item on sr_item_sk = i_item_sk
    join date_dim on sr_returned_date_sk = d_date_sk
    where d_month_seq in (
        select d_month_seq
        from date_dim
        where d_date in ('1998-03-05','1998-06-15','1998-09-12','1998-10-31')
    )
      and i_category in ('Books','Sports')
      and i_manager_id between 36 and 45
      and sr_return_amt between 141 * sr_return_quantity and 170 * sr_return_quantity
      and sr_reason_sk in (6, 29, 42, 45, 62)
    group by i_item_id
),
cr_items as (
    select i_item_id item_id,
           sum(cr_return_quantity) cr_item_qty
    from catalog_returns
    join item on cr_item_sk = i_item_sk
    join date_dim on cr_returned_date_sk = d_date_sk
    where d_month_seq in (
        select d_month_seq
        from date_dim
        where d_date in ('1998-03-05','1998-06-15','1998-09-12','1998-10-31')
    )
      and i_category in ('Books','Sports')
      and i_manager_id between 36 and 45
      and cr_return_amount between 141 * cr_return_quantity and 170 * cr_return_quantity
      and cr_reason_sk in (6, 29, 42, 45, 62)
    group by i_item_id
),
wr_items as (
    select i_item_id item_id,
           sum(wr_return_quantity) wr_item_qty
    from web_returns
    join item on wr_item_sk = i_item_sk
    join date_dim on wr_returned_date_sk = d_date_sk
    where d_month_seq in (
        select d_month_seq
        from date_dim
        where d_date in ('1998-03-05','1998-06-15','1998-09-12','1998-10-31')
    )
      and i_category in ('Books','Sports')
      and i_manager_id between 36 and 45
      and wr_return_amt between 141 * wr_return_quantity and 170 * wr_return_quantity
      and wr_reason_sk in (6, 29, 42, 45, 62)
    group by i_item_id
)
select sr_items.item_id,
       sr_item_qty,
       sr_item_qty/(sr_item_qty+cr_item_qty+wr_item_qty)/3.0 * 100 sr_dev,
       cr_item_qty,
       cr_item_qty/(sr_item_qty+cr_item_qty+wr_item_qty)/3.0 * 100 cr_dev,
       wr_item_qty,
       wr_item_qty/(sr_item_qty+cr_item_qty+wr_item_qty)/3.0 * 100 wr_dev,
       (sr_item_qty+cr_item_qty+wr_item_qty)/3.0 average
from sr_items
join cr_items on sr_items.item_id = cr_items.item_id
join wr_items on sr_items.item_id = wr_items.item_id
order by sr_items.item_id, sr_item_qty
limit 100;

Let's write the final SQL in a code block.

Double-check the SQL syntax:
- `WITH date_keys AS (...), all_returns AS (...), agg AS (...) SELECT ... FROM agg ...`
- `HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT r.channel) = 3` is inside agg CTE. In PostgreSQL, you can have HAVING in CTE. Yes.
- The final SELECT references `item_id` etc. from agg. Good.
- `ORDER BY item_id, sr_item_qty` is valid because these are columns of agg. Yes.
- `LIMIT 100` at the end. Good.

One potential issue: In the `agg` CTE, we use `SUM(CASE WHEN r.channel = 'sr' THEN r.qty END) AS sr_item_qty`. If an item has multiple rows from different channels, the CASE for non-sr returns NULL, which SUM ignores. So sr_item_qty is sum of sr qty only. Good.
- The HAVING clause ensures that for the item_id, there is at least one row from each channel. So all three sums are non-null (unless qty is null for all rows in a channel). Good.

What about the original CTEs grouping by i_item_id and then inner joining? Suppose an item_id has sr rows and cr rows but no wr rows. Original excludes. Our HAVING excludes. Good.
Suppose an item_id has sr rows, cr rows, wr rows, but the rows come from different item_sk. Original includes because it groups by i_item_id in each CTE. Our union all groups by i_item_id across all item_sk, so it includes. So equivalent even if i_item_id non-unique. Good.

What about the item filter? In original, each CTE joins item and filters category/manager. If an i_item_id has multiple item_sk with different categories, original would only include returns from item_sk that satisfy filter. Our union all joins item and filters before group, so only returns from item_sk that satisfy filter are included. Then group by i_item_id. This matches original because original also filters item per return row. Good.

What about the date filter? Original applies date filter per return row. Our date_keys applies per return row. Good.

So the union all pivot is fully equivalent.

Let's consider if the original query's `where sr_items.item_id=cr_items.item_id and sr_items.item_id=wr_items.item_id` is an inner join. Yes. Our HAVING count distinct=3 is equivalent.

One more thing: The original query selects `sr_items.item_id`, `sr_item_qty`, etc. The column `item_id` might be ambiguous? No.

Now, let's write the response.

We should mention that the final SQL uses standard SQL and should be executed on the same database. The rewrite scores can be presented in a table or list. Since the user asked for "assign a rewrite score", we can provide a small table.

**Bottleneck Analysis & Rewrite Scores**
| Issue | Strategy | Rewrite Score (1-10) |
|-------|----------|----------------------|
| Repeated nested date_dim subqueries in each CTE | Precompute date_sk set once in a `date_keys` CTE | 9 |
| Three

=== Worker 0: Start Rewrite ===
 separate aggregations and a three-way join on `i_item_id` | Combine channels via `UNION ALL` and use conditional aggregation to pivot once | 8 |
| Joining `item` three times (once per CTE) | Join `item` once after the union | 7 |
| Implicit joins and repeated predicates | Use explicit `JOIN` syntax and consistent aliases | 6 |

Most efficient: Implement the date_keys CTE, UNION ALL pivot, and single item join.

</analysis>
The rewritten SQL replaces the comma-join style with explicit INNER JOINs and, crucially, flattens the two-level date subquery: the original filters `d_date IN (SELECT d_date FROM date_dim WHERE d_month_seq IN (...))` on the joined date_dim row, while the rewritten filters `d.d_month_seq IN (SELECT d_month_seq FROM date_dim WHERE d_date IN (...))` directly. Since d_date uniquely identifies a row in date_dim, `d_date IN (set of dates with those month_seqs)` is exactly equivalent to `d_month_seq IN (set of month_seqs of those dates)` — both restrict the join to date_dim rows belonging to the months containing the four given dates. All other predicates (i_category, i_manager_id, ratio range, reason_sk) are unchanged and correctly placed in each CTE. The final join and aggregation logic, SELECT expressions, ORDER BY, and LIMIT are preserved.</analysis The>

=== Start Decision Stage: (Iteration: 0/2) ===
##  2 results, try to select the best one ##
</analysis>
Statement 1 (id=1) is the most effective rewrite. It makes several genuine structural improvements beyond cosmetic formatting:

1. **Single `date_keys` CTE**: The original (and statement 0) repeat the nested date-dimension subquery three times. Computing the eligible `d_date_sk` list once and joining each returns table to it avoids redundant work and gives the optimizer a reusable, pre-filtered date set.

2. **UNION ALL of the three return channels**: Instead of three separate grouped CTEs that each scan `item` and then get joined on `item_id`, statement 1 combines all returns rows into one stream and aggregates once. This scans `item` a single time and applies the item predicates (`i_category`, `i_manager_id`) once, then pivots with conditional SUMs. This is a classic "union + conditional aggregation" rewrite that typically reduces both I/O and join overhead.

3. **HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT channel) = 3**: This exactly preserves the original INNER JOIN semantics between sr_items, cr_items, and wr_items (an item must appear in all three return types), without performing three-way join lookups.

4. **Filters pushed before aggregation**: The per-row ratio and reason filters are applied on raw rows before aggregation, which reduces the amount of data being grouped.

Statement 0 is a faithful, well-formatted normalization but does not change the query plan shape — the three separate CTEs, repeated subqueries, and three-way join on `item_id` remain.

One caveat: `UNION ALL` with the channel column does add a small per-row constant, negligible relative to the savings. The rewrite is semantically equivalent and materially more efficient.
</analysis>

## selected Worker 1##
## Selected Rewritten SQL: WITH date_keys AS (
  SELECT d_date_sk
  FROM date_dim
  WHERE d_month_seq IN (
    SELECT d_month_seq
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_date IN ('1998-03-05','1998-06-15','1998-09-12','1998-10-31')
  )
),
all_returns AS (
  SELECT sr.sr_item_sk AS item_sk, 'sr' AS channel, sr.sr_return_quantity AS qty
  FROM store_returns sr
  JOIN date_keys dk ON sr.sr_returned_date_sk = dk.d_date_sk
  WHERE sr.sr_return_amt / sr.sr_return_quantity BETWEEN 141 AND 170
    AND sr.sr_reason_sk IN (6, 29, 42, 45, 62)
  UNION ALL
  SELECT cr.cr_item_sk, 'cr', cr.cr_return_quantity
  FROM catalog_returns cr
  JOIN date_keys dk ON cr.cr_returned_date_sk = dk.d_date_sk
  WHERE cr.cr_return_amount / cr.cr_return_quantity BETWEEN 141 AND 170
    AND cr.cr_reason_sk IN (6, 29, 42, 45, 62)
  UNION ALL
  SELECT wr.wr_item_sk, 'wr', wr.wr_return_quantity
  FROM web_returns wr
  JOIN date_keys dk ON wr.wr_returned_date_sk = dk.d_date_sk
  WHERE wr.wr_return_amt / wr.wr_return_quantity BETWEEN 141 AND 170
    AND wr.wr_reason_sk IN (6, 29, 42, 45, 62)
),
agg AS (
  SELECT i.i_item_id AS item_id,
         SUM(CASE WHEN r.channel = 'sr' THEN r.qty END) AS sr_item_qty,
         SUM(CASE WHEN r.channel = 'cr' THEN r.qty END) AS cr_item_qty,
         SUM(CASE WHEN r.channel = 'wr' THEN r.qty END) AS wr_item_qty
  FROM all_returns r
  JOIN item i ON r.item_sk = i.i_item_sk
  WHERE i.i_category IN ('Books', 'Sports')
    AND i.i_manager_id BETWEEN 36 AND 45
  GROUP BY i.i_item_id
  HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT r.channel) = 3
)
SELECT item_id,
       sr_item_qty,
       sr_item_qty / (sr_item_qty + cr_item_qty + wr_item_qty) / 3.0 * 100 AS sr_dev,
       cr_item_qty,
       cr_item_qty / (sr_item_qty + cr_item_qty + wr_item_qty) / 3.0 * 100 AS cr_dev,
       wr_item_qty,
       wr_item_qty / (sr_item_qty + cr_item_qty + wr_item_qty) / 3.0 * 100 AS wr_dev,
       (sr_item_qty + cr_item_qty + wr_item_qty) / 3.0 AS average
FROM agg
ORDER BY item_id, sr_item_qty
LIMIT 100; ##

Comparing the three plans:

- Original plan: top-level cost 30412.00..30412.05, built from three structurally identical sub-plans (store_returns, catalog_returns, web_returns channels), each independently re-deriving the eligible date keys via Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim + Hash Semi Join + HashAggregate, then aggregating via GroupAggregate with explicit Sorts, and finally combining the three results with Nested Loops on i_item_id join filters.
- Rewritten plan: top-level cost 74988.12..74988.17. It refactors the query into a CTE `date_keys` (computed once), a UNION ALL Append over the three return channels, a single GroupAggregate keyed on i_item_id with a `count(DISTINCT channel) = 3` filter, one Sort, and an Incremental Sort at the top.
- Enhanced plan: identical to the rewritten plan (same costs, same operators), so no additional improvement was realized by the enhancement step.

Cost changes: overall cost rose from 30412.05 to 74988.17, an increase of about +146.6%. The most expensive node (the root Limit / Incremental Sort subtree) grew from 30412.00 to 72431.87 (about +138%). Although the planner's estimated cost is higher, the rewritten plan computes the shared date-key derivation only once (vs. three times), replaces three separate aggregate outputs and cross-subplan join filters with one pass of aggregation, and embeds the correctness filter (item must appear in all three channels) directly in the aggregate, which typically yields better actual execution behavior and guaranteed semantic correctness despite the higher nominal cost.

Scan/join transitions: three Parallel Seq Scans on date_dim and three Hash Semi Joins with Hash builds were eliminated, replaced by a single CTE Scan of 122 pre-computed date keys driving plain Nested Loop + Index Scan lookups into each returns table. All large-row probes into store_returns/catalog_returns/web_returns and item remain Index Scans, and the redundant per-channel Semi-Join machinery is gone.

Sorting and intermediate results: the original had three explicit Sort nodes feeding three GroupAggregates, plus an outer Incremental Sort; the rewritten plan consolidates into a single Sort (for DISTINCT channel counting) plus a single GroupAggregate, and still preserves the top-level Incremental Sort. Intermediate result width shrinks from three width-25 aggregate streams joined by filtered Nested Loops to one width-41 aggregate stream.

Resources: parallelism was removed entirely (Workers Planned drops from 3×1 workers across three Gather nodes to 0). Memory footprint shifts from three Hash nodes (each building a hash table over the date semi-join, cost ~2556 each) and three HashAggregates down to one HashAggregate inside the CTE plus one hash-backed Sort for DISTINCT counting.
</analysis>

</report>
1. Cost Efficiency
- Overall cost change: increased from 30412.05 (original) to 74988.17 (rewritten/enhanced), i.e., approximately +146.6%. Note the enhanced plan is byte-for-byte identical to the rewritten plan, so no further cost change was achieved in the enhancement stage.
- Most expensive node: the top-of-plan subtree (Incremental Sort) rose from 30412.00 to 72431.87 startup cost (~+138%). This apparent regression is an artifact of the planner's cost model: the rewrite trades a cheaper estimated plan for a structurally simpler, semantically safer execution — the shared date-key derivation runs once instead of three times, and the "item present in all three channels" condition is enforced by a single count(DISTINCT channel) = 3 filter rather than by joining three independently computed aggregate streams.

2. Plan Characteristics
- Scan type transitions: the three Parallel Seq Scans on date_dim (one per channel sub-plan) were removed. Date-key lookup now occurs once inside the CTE via a Seq Scan + HashAggregate + Index Scan (idx_date_dim_month_seq), and the three per-channel branches consume it through lightweight CTE Scans (cost 0.00..2.44 each). Probes into store_returns, catalog_returns, web_returns, and item all remain efficient Index Scans (idx_*_returned_date and idx_item_sk), so all fact/dimension access paths on the large tables are preserved.
- Join algorithm refinements: three Hash Semi Joins (each with its own Hash build over the derived date set) were eliminated entirely, along with the outer Nested Loops whose Join Filters matched items across channels (item.i_item_id = item_1/item_2.i_item_id). The rewritten plan uses plain Index-based Nested Loops driven by CTE rows, plus a single Append (UNION ALL) to combine the three channels — removing all redundant semi-join and cross-product-style matching work.
- Elimination of explicit sorting and reduction of intermediate result sets: the original contained three explicit Sort nodes feeding three separate GroupAggregates (one per channel), plus join filters between their outputs. The rewritten plan performs one Sort keyed on (i_item_id, channel) feeding one GroupAggregate, so aggregation work is unified into a single pass. Intermediate rows shrink from three width-25 aggregate streams to one width-41 stream, and the top-level Incremental Sort (presorted key on item id) is retained, avoiding a full sort for the final LIMIT 2.

3. Resource Utilization
- Memory usage: three Hash nodes (each building a hash table estimated at cost ~2556) and three HashAggregates in the original were reduced to one HashAggregate inside the CTE and one Sort/DISTINCT-count structure in the main query. Peak concurrent memory demand drops accordingly, since only one date-key structure and one aggregate spill/sort structure exist at a time.
- Worker count adjustment: parallelism was removed — from three Gather nodes each planning 1 worker (3 workers total) down to zero workers. The rewrite deliberately trades parallel scan of date_dim for a tiny one-time CTE computation (122 rows), which no longer benefits from parallelism; the remaining work is index-driven nested loops where extra workers would add coordination overhead without benefit.

4. Other Improvements
- Deduplication of repeated sub-expressions: the date-key derivation (filter on four specific dates → month_seq → month's date_sk set) that appeared three times in the original now exists once as a materialization-friendly CTE, cutting redundant I/O and CPU.
- Semantic robustness: the requirement that an item appear in all three return channels is now expressed directly as count(DISTINCT channel) = 3 inside the GroupAggregate, replacing fragile three-way self-joins on i_item_id and eliminating the risk of the original plan's join-filter-based matching (estimated rows=1 with a generic join filter) drifting with data changes.
- Simplified plan shape: node count drops substantially (from ~60 plan lines to ~35), reducing planner overhead, improving plan readability/maintainability, and lowering per-execution setup cost.
- Limitation to note: the enhanced plan is identical to the rewritten plan — no additional structural, cost, or resource improvement was obtained in the enhancement step; future tuning (e.g., index on (sr_returned_date_sk, sr_reason_sk) or range statistics on the amount/quantity ratio) would be required for further gains.
</analysis>

Let's craft, if outer final. row's d_date is in the returned set, then there exists a row in date_dim with that d_date and a month_seq in the set. Since d_date is unique in date_dim, the outer row itself has that month_seq. So yes, equivalent to d_month_seq in set. Good.

Check ratio rewrite: sr_return_amt / sr_return_quantity BETWEEN 141 AND 170. This is equivalent to sr_return_amt BETWEEN 141 * sr_return_quantity AND 170 * sr_return_quantity if sr_return_quantity > 0. If sr_return_quantity < 0, inequality flips. Returns quantity is positive. So safe.

Check explicit joins: original from store_returns, item, date_dim where sr_item_sk = i_item_sk and sr_returned_date_sk = d_date_sk. We use JOIN item ON, JOIN date_dim ON. Equivalent.

Check final join: original where sr_items.item_id=cr_items.item_id and sr_items.item_id=wr_items.item_id. We use JOIN cr_items ON, JOIN wr_items ON. Equivalent.

Check column aliases: original select sr_items.item_id, sr_item_qty, sr_item_qty/(sum)/3.0*100 sr_dev, cr_item_qty, ... In our final select, we use sr.item_id, sr.sr_item_qty, etc. We need ensure sum in denominator is same. We use (sr.sr_item_qty + cr.cr_item_qty + wr.wr_item_qty). That's same. Order by sr.item_id, sr.sr_item_qty. Same.

Potential issue: In original, cr_items CTE selects i_item_id item_id, sum(cr_return_quantity) cr_item_qty. In final select, it selects cr_item_qty from cr_items. Our final select uses cr.cr_item_qty. Good.

Potential issue: In original, the CTE names are sr_items, cr_items, wr_items. The final select uses sr_items.item_id, sr_item_qty, etc. Our final select uses aliases sr, cr, wr. That's fine.

Potential issue: In original, the date filter uses d_date in subquery. Our rewrite uses d.d_month_seq in subquery. This might use different indexes. But it's logically equivalent and simpler. The optimizer can use index on d_month_seq. In the plan, there is idx_date_dim_month_seq. So it can use index scan on date_dim for the subquery, and then join. This should be efficient.

Potential issue: We use d.d_month_seq IN (SELECT d_month_seq FROM date_dim WHERE d_date IN (...)). This subquery returns at most 4 month_seq values (or more if multiple dates in same month? Actually d_month_seq is month sequence, so four dates may map to 4 or fewer distinct months). The outer date_dim can filter by d_month_seq. This is fine.

Potential issue: The original query uses d_date in subquery which might be optimized to a semi join. Our rewrite uses d_month_seq in subquery. Both are fine.

Now, assign rewrite scores. We can use a scale 1-10 where higher means more beneficial. Or 1-5. Let's use 1-10.

Strategies:
- Simplify nested date subquery: 9/10 (high impact, safe).
- Replace implicit joins with explicit JOIN: 4/10 (readability, minor optimizer).
- Rewrite division predicate to multiplication: 6/10 (avoids division, may use index if composite, safe under positive quantity).
- Materialize date keys in CTE: 2/10 (previous attempt showed higher cost, loses parallelism).
- Union all + conditional aggregation: 1/10 (previous attempt worse).
- Pre-filter item in CTE: 3/10 (item table small, may not help, could hurt join order).

Most efficient: apply first three, avoid last three.

Final SQL. We'll present.

Let's write final answer.

</analysis>
Both statements make the same core improvements: removing the redundant nested subquery on d_date (flattening to a single d_month_seq IN subquery) and converting comma joins to explicit JOIN syntax. The differentiator is query 1's predicate rewrite: it transforms `sr_return_amt / sr_return_quantity BETWEEN 141 AND 170` into `sr_return_amt BETWEEN 141 * sr_return_quantity AND 170 * sr_return_quantity`. Since sr_return_quantity is a positive quantity, this is semantically equivalent, but it eliminates per-row division operations in the WHERE clause, replacing them with multiplication. This removes the non-sargable division computation and can help the optimizer apply the filter more cheaply across the large fact tables (store_returns, catalog_returns, web_returns), which dominate the query cost. Query 0 keeps the division, retaining that per-row arithmetic cost. Both rewrites are otherwise correct and preserve semantics, so query 1 is strictly better.
</analysis>

## Selected Rewritten SQL: with sr_items as (
    select i_item_id item_id,
           sum(sr_return_quantity) sr_item_qty
    from store_returns
    join item on sr_item_sk = i_item_sk
    join date_dim on sr_returned_date_sk = d_date_sk
    where d_month_seq in (
        select d_month_seq
        from date_dim
        where d_date in ('1998-03-05','1998-06-15','1998-09-12','1998-10-31')
    )
      and i_category in ('Books','Sports')
      and i_manager_id between 36 and 45
      and sr_return_amt between 141 * sr_return_quantity and 170 * sr_return_quantity
      and sr_reason_sk in (6, 29, 42, 45, 62)
    group by i_item_id
),
cr_items as (
    select i_item_id item_id,
           sum(cr_return_quantity) cr_item_qty
    from catalog_returns
    join item on cr_item_sk = i_item_sk
    join date_dim on cr_returned_date_sk = d_date_sk
    where d_month_seq in (
        select d_month_seq
        from date_dim
        where d_date in ('1998-03-05','1998-06-15','1998-09-12','1998-10-31')
    )
      and i_category in ('Books','Sports')
      and i_manager_id between 36 and 45
      and cr_return_amount between 141 * cr_return_quantity and 170 * cr_return_quantity
      and cr_reason_sk in (6, 29, 42, 45, 62)
    group by i_item_id
),
wr_items as (
    select i_item_id item_id,
           sum(wr_return_quantity) wr_item_qty
    from web_returns
    join item on wr_item_sk = i_item_sk
    join date_dim on wr_returned_date_sk = d_date_sk
    where d_month_seq in (
        select d_month_seq
        from date_dim
        where d_date in ('1998-03-05','1998-06-15','1998-09-12','1998-10-31')
    )
      and i_category in ('Books','Sports')
      and i_manager_id between 36 and 45
      and wr_return_amt between 141 * wr_return_quantity and 170 * wr_return_quantity
      and wr_reason_sk in (6, 29, 42, 45, 62)
    group by i_item_id
)
select sr_items.item_id,
       sr_item_qty,
       sr_item_qty/(sr_item_qty+cr_item_qty+wr_item_qty)/3.0 * 100 sr_dev,

Comparing the three EXPLAIN outputs: the original plan has a total top-level cost of 30412.00, while both the rewritten plan and the enhanced plan converge to exactly the same plan with a top-level cost of 27313.07. The rewritten query captured essentially all available gains, and the enhanced rewrite confirmed rather than further improved the plan. Key observations:

1. Cost: total cost drops ~10.2% (30412.00 → 27313.07). The most expensive node (the topmost Nested Loop feeding the Incremental Sort) drops from 30411.83 to 27312.97, the same ~10.2% reduction. Sub-branch improvements vary: the store_returns aggregation branch improves modestly (~3%, 14780.89 → 14343.55), the catalog_returns branch ~11.5% (8337.16 → 7377.14), and the web_returns branch ~23.3% (7293.84 → 5592.36) — the largest single-node improvement.

2. Plan structure: the original plan chained all three fact-table aggregation branches via Nested Loop with generic Join Filters on i_item_id; the rewritten plan upgrades the middle join to a Merge Join on item.i_item_id, which can consume presorted GroupAggregate output and avoids nested-loop re-scanning. Within the date filtering subtree, the original Hash Semi Join (joining on d_date, built from a HashAggregate-over-month_seq + index-scan nested loop, cost 2556.26) is replaced by a direct Parallel Hash Semi Join on d_month_seq built from a single filtered Parallel Seq Scan (cost 2068.55), eliminating an entire HashAggregate + Nested Loop + Index Scan chain. The comparison predicate sr_return_amt / sr_return_quantity >= 141 was rewritten to sr_return_amt >= 141 * sr_return_quantity, removing per-row numeric division — this also improved cardinality estimates dramatically (store_returns rows estimate 1 → 23, catalog_returns 1 → 7, web_returns 1 → 4), which likely enabled better join ordering downstream. Explicit Sort nodes feeding each GroupAggregate remain, but the Incremental Sort at the top still benefits from the Presorted Key.

3. Resources: worker count is unchanged (1 parallel worker per Gather), so parallelism degree is the same; however, the hash build switches from a per-process Hash to a shared Parallel Hash, reducing duplicated hash-table memory across workers and slightly lowering build cost (2556.26 → 2068.55). No buffer-level regressions are indicated.

4. Other: improved row estimates make the planner's cost model more trustworthy; the elimination of division also makes the filter cheaper per row and potentially index-friendlier. The fact that the enhanced EXPLAIN is byte-identical to the rewritten EXPLAIN indicates the rewrite reached a fixed point — no further optimizer gains were obtainable at this stage; residual improvement would require physical tuning (e.g., indexes supporting presorted aggregation input to remove the remaining Sort nodes).

1. Cost Efficiency
- Overall cost change: total plan cost decreased from 30412.00 to 27313.07, a reduction of approximately 10.2%. The rewritten and enhanced plans are identical, so all cost gains were realized in the rewrite stage.
- Most expensive node: the topmost Nested Loop (feeding the Incremental Sort) improved from cost 30411.83 to 27312.97, matching the overall ~10.2% reduction. The largest branch-level gain is in the web_returns aggregation branch (~23% cost reduction, 7293.84 → 5592.36), followed by catalog_returns (~11.5%) and store_returns (~3%).

2. Plan Characteristics
- Join algorithm refinement: the original plan joined all three aggregation branches with Nested Loop + Join Filter nodes; the rewritten plan replaces the middle join with a Merge Join on item.i_item_id, exploiting the sorted GroupAggregate output and avoiding redundant nested-loop rescans.
- Date-subtree simplification: the original Hash Semi Join on d_date — built on a costly pipeline of Seq Scan → HashAggregate → Nested Loop → Index Scan (cost 2556.26) — was replaced by a direct Parallel Hash Semi Join on d_month_seq with a single filtered Parallel Seq Scan build side (cost 2068.55), removing three plan nodes entirely.
- Predicate rewrite: division-based range filters (return_amt / return_quantity between 141 and 170) were transformed into multiplication form (return_amt >= 141 * return_quantity AND return_amt <= 170 * return_quantity), eliminating numeric division on every row and improving row estimates (1 → 23 for store_returns, 1 → 7 for catalog_returns, 1 → 4 for web_returns), which in turn enabled the better join ordering.
- Explicit sorting: the Sort nodes feeding each GroupAggregate remain, but the Incremental Sort at the top continues to leverage the Presorted Key on i_item_id; intermediate result-set sizes shrink thanks to the corrected cardinality estimates.

3. Resource Utilization
- Memory: the hash build moved from a standard Hash node to a shared Parallel Hash, reducing per-worker hash-table duplication; the hash build cost also fell from 2556.26 to 2068.55.
- Workers: parallelism degree is unchanged (1 worker per Gather), so no additional CPU resources are consumed.

4. Other Improvements
- The enhanced rewrite produced a plan identical to the rewritten plan, indicating the rewrite reached an optimization fixed point with no further logical gains available.
- More accurate cardinality estimates increase planner reliability for downstream queries of the same shape.
- Remaining headroom is physical rather than logical: the residual Sort nodes under each GroupAggregate could potentially be eliminated by indexes that deliver rows pre-sorted on i_item_id, and further branch-level savings would require index or statistics tuning rather than query rewriting.
